{{(\theta(g))}_{A}^{-1}}(\phi(g(u)))\neq\emptyset
.

Since
\phi(g(u))\in{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{2}}}}
and
\operatorname{Im}\theta={{T}_{2}}
,
\exists v\in V
and
h\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
such that
\phi(g(u))=\theta({{g}^{h}})(v)
. If
h=1
, then by
(
7.12
)
(with
f:=g
and
x:=\phi(g(u))
),
v\in{{(\theta(g))}_{A}^{-1}}(\phi(g(u)))
. And if
h>1
, then because
\theta
is distributive over
V\bigcup{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{2}}}}
,
\phi(g(u))=\theta({{g}^{h}})(v)=\theta(g)(\theta({{g}^{h-1}})(v))
, and hence by
(
7.12
)
(with
f:=g
,
x:=\phi(g(u))
and the fact that
\theta({{g}^{h-1}})(v)\in{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{2}}}}\subseteq A
),
\theta({{g}^{h-1}})(v)\in{{(\theta(g))}_{A}^{-1}}(\phi(g(u)))
. In both cases of
h
,
{{(\theta(g))}_{A}^{-1}}(\phi(g(u)))\neq\emptyset
, as desired. Therefore,
{{A}_{u}}\neq\emptyset
, as desired.

In summary, to define a map
\alpha:U\to V\bigcup{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{2}}}}
, for each
u\in U
, let
\alpha(u)
be any element of
{A}_{u}
. Then
\alpha
satisfies
\phi(f(u))=\theta(f)(\alpha(u))
,
\forall u\in U
and
f\in{{T}_{1}}
. Hence by Definition
7.3.7
,
\phi
is constructible by
\alpha
.
∎

7.4.
Another construction of
T
-morphisms

Let
\sigma
be a
T
-morphism from a
T
-space
S
. If
\sigma(a)=b
, then
\forall f\in T
,
\sigma(f(a))=f(\sigma(a))=f(b)
. In other words, if an ordered pair
(a,b)\in\sigma
, where
\sigma
is regarded as a binary relation, then
\forall f\in T
,
(f(a),f(b))\in\sigma
. This observation implies that there exists some sort of basic parts of
T
-morphisms.

To facilitate our descriptions, we give the following notations only for this subsection.

Notation 7.4.1
.

Let
T^{*}=T\bigcup\{
Id on
D
} and let
{{R}_{T}}(x,y)=\{(f(x),f(y))\,|\,f\in T^{*}\}
, where
(x,y)\in D\times D
. Besides, both
{{S}_{a}}
and
{{S}_{b}}
denote
T
-spaces.

Remark
.

Clearly,
{{R}_{T}}(x,y)\subseteq D\times D
is a binary relation in
D
.

Proposition 7.4.2
.

Let
R\subseteq{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
such that
\operatorname{Dom}R
is a
T
-subspace of
S_{a}
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

R
is a
T
-morphism from
\operatorname{Dom}R
to
{{S}_{b}}
.

(ii)

There exists a binary relation
\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{\{({{a}_{i}},{{b}_{i}})\}}\subseteq{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
such that
R=\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{{{R}_{T}}({{a}_{i}},{{b}_{i}})}
and
R
is a function.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Suppose
R=\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{\{({{a}_{i}},{{b}_{i}})}\}
. By Definition
2.3.1
,
\forall f\in T^{*}
and
({{a}_{i}},{{b}_{i}})\in R
,
R(f({{a}_{i}}))=f(R({{a}_{i}}))=f({{b}_{i}})
, and hence
(f({{a}_{i}}),f({{b}_{i}}))\in R
.

Therefore,
R=\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{\{(f({{a}_{i}}),f({{b}_{i}}))\,|\,f\in T^{*}\}}=\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{{{R}_{T}}({{a}_{i}},{{b}_{i}})}
. Hence (ii) is true.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Let
g\in T^{*}
. Suppose
(u,v)\in R
.

Since
R=(\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{{{R}_{T}}({{a}_{i}},{{b}_{i}})}=)\bigcup\nolimits_{i}{\{(f({{a}_{i}}),f({{b}_{i}}))}\,|\,f\in T^{*}\}
and
T
is a semigroup,
(g(u),g(v))\in R
. Because
R
is a function,
R(g(u))=g(v)=g(R(u))
. Hence by Definition
2.3.1
,
R
is a
T
-morphism from
\operatorname{Dom}R
to
{{S}_{b}}
. ∎

Proposition
7.4.2
implies that
{{R}_{T}}(x,y)
, where
(x,y)\in{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
, may be regarded as some sort of basic part of a
T
-morphism from a
T
-subspace of
{{S}_{a}}
to
{{S}_{b}}
. This observation inspires us to define a topology on
{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
as follows.

Definition 7.4.3
.

Let
{B_{T}}({S_{a}}\times{S_{b}})=\{{R_{T}}(x,y)\,|\,(x,y)\in{S_{a}}\times{S_{b}}\}
. Let
{{\mathcal{T}}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
be the topology on
{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
generated by
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
as a subbasis; that is, let
{{\mathcal{T}}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
be the collection of all unions of finite intersections of elements of
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
.

Proposition 7.4.4
.

Let
\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}
be an arbitrary intersection of elements
B_{i}
of
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
. Then
\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}
is also a union of elements of
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
.

Proof.

If
\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}=\emptyset
, then by convention,
\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}
may be regarded as the union of an empty family of elements of
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
. So we only need to consider the case where
\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}\neq\emptyset
.

Any element
B_{i}
of
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
is a set
{R_{T}}(x,y)=\{(f(x),f(y))\,|\,f\in T^{*}\text{ }\!\!\}\!\!\text{ }
, where
(x,y)\in{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
. So for any
(u,v)\in B_{i}
and
g\in T^{*}
, since
T
is a semigroup, we can tell
(g(u),g(v))\in B_{i}
. Then
\forall g\in T^{*}
and
(u,v)\in\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}
,
(g(u),g(v))\in\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}
.

Therefore,

\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}

=\bigcup\nolimits_{(u,v)\in\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}}{\{(g(u),g(v))\,|\,g\in T^{*}\text{ }\!\!\}\!\!\text{ }}

=\bigcup\nolimits_{(u,v)\in\bigcap\nolimits_{i}{B_{i}}}{{{R}_{T}}(u,v)}.

∎

Thus immediately we have

Corollary 7.4.5
.

{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
is a basis of
{{\mathcal{T}}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
. Thus,
{{\mathcal{T}}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
is the collection of all unions of elements of
{{B}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
.

Combining Proposition
7.4.2
with the above corollary, we immediately obtain the main theorem of this subsection as follows.

Theorem 7.4.6
.

Let
R\subseteq{{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}}
such that
\operatorname{Dom}R
is a
T
-subspace of
S_{a}
. Then
R
is a
T
-morphism from
\operatorname{Dom}R
to
{{S}_{b}}
if and only if
R\in{{\mathcal{T}}_{T}}({{S}_{a}}\times{{S}_{b}})
and
R
is a function.

Part II. THEORY FOR THE CASE OF MULTIVARIABLE TOTAL OR PARTIAL FUNCTIONS

To make our theory more general, in Section
8
, we shall generalize the notion of operator semigroup to incorporate functions of more than one variable. Then we shall find that some familiar concepts such as ring homomorphisms, module homomorphisms, and group homomorphisms can be characterized by
T
-morphisms or
\theta
-morphisms. Moreover, in Section
9
, we shall allow elements in
T
to be partial functions. Then we will find that some notions such as covariant functors between categories can be characterized in terms of
T
-morphisms or
\theta
-morphisms.

8.
Basic notions for the case of multivariable (total) functions

Subsection
8.1
generalizes the notion of operator semigroup to incorporate multivariable functions. Accordingly, Subsections
8.2
,
8.3
,
8.4
,
8.5
and
8.6
generalize the notions of
T
-spaces,
T
-morphisms,
T
-isomorphisms,
\theta
-morphisms and
\theta
-isomorphisms, respectively.

8.1.
Operator generalized-semigroup
T

Definition 8.1.1
.

Let
D
be a set, let
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
, and let
f
be a function from the cartesian product
{{D}^{n}}
to
D
.
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
, let
n_{i}\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
and let
{{g}_{i}}
be a function from the cartesian product
{{D}^{{{n}_{i}}}}
to
D
. Let
m=\sum\nolimits_{i}{{{n}_{i}}}
. Then the
composite

f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})
is the function from
{{D}^{m}}
to
D
given by

({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{m}})\mapsto f({{g}_{1}}({{\text{v}}_{1}}),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}({{\text{v}}_{n}})),

where
{{\text{v}}_{i}}:=({{x}_{{{m}_{i}}+1}},\cdots,{{x}_{{{m}_{i}}+{{n}_{i}}}})\in{{D}^{{{n}_{i}}}},\forall i=1,\cdots,n
,
{{m}_{1}}:=0
and
{{m}_{i}}:=\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{i-1}{{{n}_{k}}},\forall i=2,\cdots,n
.

Remark
.

If
n=1
, we can tell that the
f\circ g_{1}
defined above is the usual composite of functions
f
and
g_{1}
.

Now Definition
2.1.1
is generalized to

Definition 8.1.2
.

Let
D
be a set and let
T
be a subset of

{all functions from the cartesian product
{{D}^{n}}
to
D\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
}.

If
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
of
n
variables, and
({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})\in{{T}^{n}}
(cartesian product), the composite
f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})\in T
, then we call
T
an
operator generalized-semigroup
, abbreviated as
operator gen-semigroup
, on
D
and
D
is called the
domain
of
T
.

Remark
.

(1)

The terminology “generalized-semigroup” is informal and just for convenience because we have not defined it.

(2)

Obviously, any operator semigroup is an operator gen-semigroup.

The following generalizes Example
2.1.2
.

Example 8.1.3
.

Let
B
be a subfield of a field
F
. Let
T
be the set

\{f^{*}:{{F}^{n}}\to F
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}],n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
,

where
B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
is the ring of polynomials over
B
in
n
variables.

Then by Definition
8.1.1
,
\forall f^{*},{{g}_{1}}^{*},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}^{*}\in T
such that
f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
and
{{g}_{i}}\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{{{n}_{i}}}}]
,
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
, the composite
f^{*}\circ({{g}_{1}}^{*},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}^{*})
can be given by

({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{m}})\mapsto f({{g}_{1}}({{\text{v}}_{1}}),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}({{\text{v}}_{n}}))
,

where
m:=\sum\nolimits_{i}{{{n}_{i}}}
,
({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{m}})\in{{F}^{m}}
,
{{\text{v}}_{i}}:=({{x}_{{{m}_{i}}+1}},\cdots,{{x}_{{{m}_{i}}+{{n}_{i}}}})\in{{F}^{{{n}_{i}}}},\forall i=1,\cdots,n
,
{{m}_{1}}:=0
and
{{m}_{i}}:=\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{i-1}{{{n}_{k}}},\forall i=2,\cdots,n
.

For example, let
f={{g}_{1}}={{g}_{2}}={{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}}
. Then
f^{*}\circ({{g}_{1}}^{*},{{g}_{2}}^{*})
can be induced by (with a slight abuse of notation)
f({{g}_{1}},{{g}_{2}})=({{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}})({{x}_{3}}{{x}_{4}})={{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}}{{x}_{3}}{{x}_{4}}
. That is,
f^{*}\circ({{g}_{1}}^{*},{{g}_{2}}^{*}):{{F}^{4}}\to F
is given by
({{a}_{1}},{{a}_{2}},{{a}_{3}},{{a}_{4}})\mapsto{{a}_{1}}{{a}_{2}}{{a}_{3}}{{a}_{4}},\forall({{a}_{1}},{{a}_{2}},{{a}_{3}},{{a}_{4}})\in{{F}^{4}}
.

Then from Definition
8.1.2
, we can tell that
T
is an operator gen-semigroup on
F
.

Note that the map which induces
T
, i.e.

\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]}\to T

given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
, is surjective, but it is not necessarily injective.

The following several examples will also be employed.

Example 8.1.4
.

Let
R
be a ring with identity and let
{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle
be the ring of polynomials over the prime field of order 2 in
n
noncommuting variables. Let

T=\{f^{*}:{{R}^{n}}\to R
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Then we can tell that
T
is an operator gen-semigroup on
R
.

The map which induces
T
, i.e.

\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle}\to T

is given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
.

Example 8.1.5
.

Let
M
be a module over a commutative ring
R
with identity. Let

T=\{f^{*}:{{M}^{n}}\to M
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
,

(where
\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\in R[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
). Then
T
is an operator gen-semigroup on
M
.

Let
\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}}\to T
be the map inducing
T
given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
.

Example 8.1.6
.

Let
G
be an abelian group. Let

T=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{G}^{n}}\to G}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z},\forall i=1,\cdots,n\}
},

(where
\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\in\operatorname{Frac}({{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}])
). Then
T
is an operator gen-semigroup on
G
.

Let
\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}}\to T
be the map inducing
T
given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
.

For not necessarily abelian groups, we have

Example 8.1.7
.

Let
G
be a group. Recall that a word is a finite string of symbols, where repetition is allowed. Let
\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle
denote the set of all words on
\{{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\}
. Let

T=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{G}^{n}}\to G}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle\}
.

Then we can tell that
T
is an operator gen-semigroup on
G
.

Let
\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle}\to T
be the map inducing
T
given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
.

8.2.
T
-spaces

Definition 8.2.1
.

Let
T
be an operator gen-semigroup on
D
and let
U\subseteq D
.
Then we call

S:=\{f(u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
has
n
variables, and
(u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})\in U^{n}\}

the
T
-space
generated by
U
and denote
S
by
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
.

Example 8.2.2
.

Let
B
be a subfield of a field
F
. Let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined in Example
8.1.3
. Then
\forall(\emptyset\neq)U\subseteq F
,
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
is the ring
B[U]
.

Example 8.2.3
.

Let
R
be a ring with identity. Let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup defined in Example
8.1.4
. Then
\forall(\emptyset\neq)U\subseteq R
,
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a subring of
R
. Specifically,
{{\left\langle R\right\rangle}_{T}}=R
because
\operatorname{Id}\in T
.

Example 8.2.4
.

Let
M
be a module over a commutative ring
R
with identity. Let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup defined in Example
8.1.5
. If
X
is a nonempty subset of
M
, then
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
is the submodule of
M
generated by
X
.

Example 8.2.5
.

Let
G
be an abelian group. Let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup defined in Example
8.1.6
. If
X
is a nonempty subset of
G
, then
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
is the subgroup of
G
generated by
X
.

Example 8.2.6
.

Let
G
be a group. Let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup defined in Example
8.1.7
. If
X
is a nonempty subset of
G
, then
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
is the subgroup of
G
generated by
X
.

8.3.
T
-morphisms

Definition 8.3.1
.

Let
T
be an operator gen-semigroup and let
\sigma
be a map from a
T
-space
S
to a
T
-space. If
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in T
of
n
variables,

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})),

then we call
\sigma
a
T
-morphism
.

Remark
.

From Definitions
8.1.2
and
8.2.1
(or from Proposition
10.1.1
, which generalizes Proposition
2.2.7
), we can tell
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in S
. Hence
\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
is well-defined.

Note that in Examples
8.1.3
to
8.1.7
, all operator gen-semigroups are induced from polynomials, rational functions, or words on a set. Therefore, we introduce an (informal) notion as follows, which will facilitate our study on
T
-morphisms and
\theta
-morphisms (defined later).

Definition 8.3.2
.

Let
f
be an expression associated with a rule
R
. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

If all of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
arise as symbols in
f
, and there exist sets
C
and
D
such that, by the rule
R
,
f
yields a single element, which we denote by
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
, in
C
whenever every
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
in
f
is replaced by any
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in D
, respectively, then we call the couple
(f,R)
a (C-valued)
formal function
of
n
variables
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
and
D
is called a
domain
of
(f,R)
.

Moreover, we may call
f
a
formal function
if its associated rule
R
is clear from the context.

Remark
.

(1)

For example,
1+0{{x}_{1}}+0{{x}_{2}}\in\mathbb{Q}[{{x}_{1}},{{x}_{2}}]
is a formal function of two variables with the associated rule being the addition and multiplication of field elements. In Examples
8.1.3
to
8.1.7
, all operator gen-semigroups are induced from formal functions.

(2)

By the definition, a formal function
(f,R)
may have more than one domain. For example, any field which contains
\mathbb{Q}
is a domain of any formal function
f\in\mathbb{Q}[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
.

(3)

Any function
f
of
n
variables
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
may be regarded as the formal function
f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
, which is an expression with its associated rule being the mapping defined by the function
f
. The converse is not true, however, because a formal function may have more than one domain, and different domains of a formal function may induce different functions. Therefore, the notion of formal function generalizes the notion of function.

When operator gen-semigroups are induced from formal functions, as is the case in Examples
8.1.3
to
8.1.7
, the following lemma will be useful.

Lemma 8.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of D-valued formal functions on a domain
D
and let
T
be an operator gen-semigroup on
D
induced by
\mathcal{F}
; that is, there is a surjective map
\tau:\mathcal{F}\to T
given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
, where
f^{*}:{{D}^{n}}\to D
is given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
for
f
of
n
variables,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
. Let
\sigma
be a map from a
T
-space
S
to a
T
-space. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\sigma
is a
T
-morphism.

(ii)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
of
n
variables,

(8.1)

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): By Definition
8.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
of
n
variables,

\sigma(\tau(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=\tau(f)(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

Then by the definition of
\tau
, (ii) must be true.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Since
\tau
is surjective,
\forall f^{*}\in T
,
\exists f\in\mathcal{F}
such that
f^{*}=\tau(f)
. Then from statement (ii) and the definition of
\tau
, we can tell that
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f^{*}\in T
of
n
variables,
\sigma(f^{*}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f^{*}(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
. Hence by Definition
8.3.1
,
\sigma
must be a
T
-morphism. ∎

Lemma
8.3.3
characterizes a
T
-morphism by the formal functions which induce
T
. When an operator gen-semigroup is induced from formal functions, the criterion for a
T
-morphism by Lemma
8.3.3
is normally more convenient than that by Definition
8.3.1
. Thus Lemma
8.3.3
simplifies our proofs of some of our results as follows.

The following several results serve as examples of
T
-morphisms.

Firstly, Proposition
2.3.2
is generalized to

Proposition 8.3.4
.

Let
B
be a subfield of a field
F
, let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined in Example
8.1.3
, and let nonempty
U,V\subseteq F
. Then a map
\sigma:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely if and only if it is a
T
-morphism.

Proof.

We may give a proof which is analogous to that of Proposition
2.3.2
. However, now we employ Lemma
8.3.3
instead.
By Example
8.1.3
,

T=\{f^{*}:{{F}^{n}}\to F
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}],n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Thus
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
are the rings
B[U]
and
B[V]
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\{f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
. Then Lemma
8.3.3
applies. Hence we only need to show that
\sigma:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.3.3
is true in this case. Specifically, it suffices to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall c\in B
,
\sigma(c)=c
, and
\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}(=B[U])
,

\sigma(a+b)=\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)
and
\sigma(ab)=\sigma(a)\sigma(b)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle U\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
and
f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
,

(8.2)

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Since
f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
is a polynomial, it is not hard to see that statement (2) is true.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Considering the case where
f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
is a constant polynomial, we can tell from statement (2) that
\forall c\in B
,
\sigma(c)=c
.

Let
a,b\in{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
. Let
g={{x}_{1}}+{{x}_{2}}
and
h={{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}}
. Then

\sigma(a+b)

=\sigma(g(a,b))

=g(\sigma(a),\sigma(b))
(by Equation
(
8.2
)
)

=\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)
.

And

\sigma(ab)

=\sigma(h(a,b))

=h(\sigma(a),\sigma(b))
(by Equation
(
8.2
)
)

=\sigma(a)\sigma(b)
. ∎

However, ring homomorphism in Proposition
8.3.4
is between extensions over the same field. We will deal with ring homomorphisms between extensions over different fields in Subsection
8.5
.

Proposition 8.3.5
.

Let
M
be a module over a commutative ring
R
with identity. Let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup defined in Example
8.1.5
. If
X
and
Y
are nonempty subsets of
M
, then a map
\sigma:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
is an
R
-homomorphism of
R
-modules if and only if it is a
T
-morphism.

Proof.

By Example
8.1.5
,

T=\{f^{*}:{{M}^{n}}\to M
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

So
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
are the submodules of
M
generated by
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\{f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}\ |\ n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
. Then Lemma
8.3.3
applies. Thus we only need to show that
\sigma:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
is an
R
-homomorphism of
R
-modules if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.3.3
is true in this case. Specifically, it suffices to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
r\in R
,
\sigma(a+b)=\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)
and
\sigma(ra)=r\sigma(a)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle X\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
and
f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}
,

(8.3)

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Because
f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}
is a linear polynomial whose constant term is zero, it is not hard to see that statement (2) is true.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Let
a,b\in{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
r\in R
. Let
g={{x}_{1}}+{{x}_{2}}
and
h=r{{x}_{1}}
. Then

\sigma(a+b)

=\sigma(g(a,b))

=g(\sigma(a),\sigma(b))
(by Equation
(
8.3
)
)

=\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)
.

And

\sigma(ra)

=\sigma(h(a))

=h(\sigma(a))
(by Equation
(
8.3
)
)

=r\sigma(a)
. ∎

R
-homomorphism in Proposition
8.3.5
is between submodules of the same
R
-module. We will deal with general
R
-homomorphisms of
R
-modules in Subsection
8.5
.

Proposition 8.3.6
.

Let
G
be an abelian group and let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup on
G
defined in Example
8.1.6
. If
X
and
Y
are nonempty subsets of
G
, then a map
\sigma:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a group homomorphism if and only if it is a
T
-morphism.

Proof.

By Example
8.1.6
,

T=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{G}^{n}}\to G}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
}.

Obviously,
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
are the subgroups of
G
generated by
X
and
Y
, respectively.

We may apply Lemma
8.3.3
as well in this proof. However, our argument as follows seems more straightforward.

Suppose that
\sigma:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a
T
-morphism. Then by Definition
8.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle X\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
and
f\in T
of
n
variables,

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

In words,
\sigma
commutes with the (multiplicative) operation equipped by
G
. Hence
\sigma
is a group homomorphism.

Apparently the converse of the above argument is also true. That is, if
\sigma
is a group homomorphism, then
\sigma
commutes with the operation equipped by
G
, and hence it is a
T
-morphism. ∎

Group homomorphism in Proposition
8.3.6
is between subgroups of the same abelian group. We will deal with group homomorphisms between different abelian groups in Subsection
8.5
.

For not necessarily abelian groups, we have

Proposition 8.3.7
.

Let
G
be a group and let
T
be the operator gen-semigroup on
G
defined in Example
8.1.7
. If
X
and
Y
are nonempty subsets of
G
, then a map
\sigma:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a group homomorphism if and only if it is a
T
-morphism.

Proof.

The proof is comparable with that of Proposition
8.3.6
.

By Example
8.1.7
,

T=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{G}^{n}}\to G}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle\}
,

Obviously,
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
are the subgroups of
G
generated by
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Suppose that
\sigma:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a
T
-morphism. Then by Definition
8.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle X\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
and
f\in T
of
n
variables,

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

In words,
\sigma
commutes with the multiplication equipped by
G
. Hence
\sigma
is a group homomorphism.

It is not hard to see that the converse of the above argument is also true. Specifically, if
\sigma
is a group homomorphism, then
\sigma
commutes with the multiplication equipped by
G
, and hence it is a
T
-morphism. ∎

Group homomorphism in Proposition
8.3.7
is between subgroups of the same group. We will deal with general group homomorphisms in Subsection
8.5
.

8.4.
T
-isomorphisms

Definition
2.4.1
still applies: if a
T
-morphism is bijective, then we call it a
T
-isomorphism
. To justify the definition, we need to show Proposition
2.4.2
with
T
being an operator gen-semigroup as follows.

Proposition 8.4.1
.

(Proposition
2.4.2
)

Let
\sigma
be a
T
-isomorphism from a
T
-space
S_{1}
to a
T
-space
S_{2}
and let
{{\sigma}^{-1}}
be the inverse map of
\sigma
. Then
{{\sigma}^{-1}}\in\operatorname{Iso}_{T}({{S}_{2}},{{S}_{1}})
.

Proof.

By Definition
8.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
of
n
variables and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{S_{1}^{n}}
,

f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})={\sigma}^{-1}(f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))).

So
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
of
n
variables and
(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})\in{S_{2}^{n}}
,

f({\sigma}^{-1}({b}_{1}),\cdots,{\sigma}^{-1}({b}_{n}))={\sigma}^{-1}(f(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})).

Then by Definition
8.3.1
,
{\sigma}^{-1}
is a
T
-morphism from
S_{2}
to
S_{1}
, and hence
{{\sigma}^{-1}}\in\operatorname{Iso}_{T}({{S}_{2}},{{S}_{1}})
by Definition
2.4.1
.
∎

8.5.
\theta
-morphisms

To generalize Definition
6.1.8
, we first generalize Notation
6.1.4
to

Notation 8.5.1
.

Let
D_{1}
(resp.
D_{2}
) be a set, let
M_{1}
(resp.
M_{2}
) be a subset of {all functions from the cartesian product
D_{1}^{n}
to
{D_{1}}\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
} (resp. a subset of {all functions from
D_{2}^{n}
to
{{D}_{2}}\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
}), let
\theta\subseteq{{M}_{1}}\times{{M}_{2}}
, and let
A\subseteq{{D}_{2}}
. We denote by
\theta{{|}_{A}}
the binary relation
\{(f,g{{|}_{A}})\,|\,(f,g)\in\theta\}
, where
g{{|}_{A}}
denotes the function obtained by restricting every variable of
g
to
A
.

Definition
6.1.5
is generalized to

Definition 8.5.2
.

Let
\theta
and
A
be defined as in Notation
8.5.1
. Let
f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
,
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
.
If
\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta
,
g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
, then we say that
\theta(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is
well-defined
and let

\theta(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}).

Remark
.

By Convention
6.1.7
,

\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta,g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})

implies that
\forall(f,g)\in\theta
,
g
has
n
variables.

Hence Proposition
6.1.6
is generalized to

Proposition 8.5.3
.

Let
\theta
and
A
be defined as in Notation
8.5.1
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\theta{{|}_{A}}
is a map.

(ii)

\forall f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
,
\exists n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
such that
\forall(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
,
\theta(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined.

Proof.

\theta{{|}_{A}}
is a map;

\Leftrightarrow

\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta
,
g_{1}{{|}_{A}}=g_{2}{{|}_{A}}
;

\Leftrightarrow

\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta
,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
have the same number of variables, assumed
n
, and
\forall(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
,
g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
;

\Leftrightarrow

\forall f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
,
\exists n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
such that
\forall(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
,
\theta(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined.
∎

Then Definition
6.1.8
is generalized to

Definition 8.5.4
.

Let
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
be operator gen-semigroups and let
\theta\subseteq{{T}_{1}}\times{{T}_{2}}
. Let
\phi
be a map from a
T_{1}
-space
S
to a
T_{2}
-space. If

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
of
n
variables,

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=\theta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})),

then
\phi
is called a
\theta
-morphism
.

Then the first half of Corollary
6.1.9
is generalized to

Corollary 8.5.5
.

Let
\phi
be a
\theta
-morphism from a
T_{1}
-space
S
. Then
\forall(f,g)\in\theta
,
f
and
g
have the same number of variables, and
\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
is a map.

Proof.

Suppose
n
-variable
f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
. Then by Definition
8.5.4
and Convention
6.1.7
,
\theta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined. Hence by Definition
8.5.2
,
\forall(f,g)\in\theta
,
g
has
n
variables.
Moreover, by Proposition
8.5.3
,
\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
is a map.
∎

When operator gen-semigroups are induced from formal functions, the following, which generalizes Lemma
8.3.3
, will facilitate study of
\theta
-morphisms.

Lemma 8.5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}

(
resp.
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})
be a set of D-valued
(
resp.
{D}^{\prime}
-valued
)
formal functions on
D

(
resp.
{D}^{\prime})
. Let
T

(
resp.
{T}^{\prime})
be an operator gen-semigroup on
D

(
resp.
{D}^{\prime}
)
such that there is a map
\tau:\mathcal{F}\to T
being given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
, where
f^{*}:{{D}^{n}}\to D
is given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
for
f
of
n
variables,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}

(
resp. such that there is a map
{\tau}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\to{T}^{\prime}
being given analogously
)
. Let
\vartheta:\mathcal{F}\to{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
be a map. Let
\theta=\{(\tau(f),{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(f)))\,|\,f\in\mathcal{F}\}
and let
\phi
be a map from a
T
-space
S
to a
{T}^{\prime}
-space.

Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism.

(ii)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
of n variables,

(8.4)

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=\vartheta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})).

Proof.

Obviously,
\theta\subseteq T\times T^{\prime}
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii):
Assume
n
-variable
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
. Then

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\phi(\tau(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\theta(\tau(f))(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
(because
\tau(f)\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
and by Definition
8.5.4
)

={\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(f))(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
(because
(\tau(f),{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(f)))\in\theta
and by Corollary
8.5.5
,
\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
is a map)

=\vartheta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
, as desired.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i):
To show that
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism, we first need to show that
\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
is a map.

Let
(h,g{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}),(h,{g}^{\prime}{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}})\in\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
with
(h,g),(h,{g}^{\prime})\in\theta
. By the definition of
\theta
,
\exists l,m\in\mathcal{F}
such that
(h,g)=(\tau(l),{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(l)))
and
(h,{g}^{\prime})=(\tau(m),{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(m)))
, and hence
\tau(l)=\tau(m)
. Thus
l
and
m
have the same number of variables, which we assume to be
n
. Then by Equation (
8.4
) and Convention
6.1.7
, both
\vartheta(l)
and
\vartheta(m)
have
n
variables.

Assume
g{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}\neq{g}^{\prime}{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
; that is,
{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(l)){{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}\neq{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(m)){{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
. Then

\exists({{z}_{1}},\cdots,{{z}_{n}})\in{{S}^{n}}
such that

\vartheta(l)(\phi({{z}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{z}_{n}}))\neq\vartheta(m)(\phi({{z}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{z}_{n}})).

However,
\phi(l({{z}_{1}},\cdots,{{z}_{n}}))=\phi(m({{z}_{1}},\cdots,{{z}_{n}}))
because
\tau(l)=\tau(m)
, and hence from Equation
(
8.4
)
, we can tell

\vartheta(l)(\phi({{z}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{z}_{n}}))=\vartheta(m)(\phi({{z}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{z}_{n}})),

a contradiction.

Hence
\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
must be a map. Then
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
of
n
variables,

\phi(\tau(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
(by the definition of
\tau
)

=\vartheta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
(by Equation (
8.4
))

={\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(f))(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
(by the definition of
{\tau}^{\prime}
)

=\theta(\tau(f))(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
(because
(\tau(f),{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(f)))\in\theta
and
\theta{{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}}
is a map).

Since
\operatorname{Dom}\theta=\{\tau(f)\,|\,f\in\mathcal{F}\}
, by Definition
8.5.4
,
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism. ∎

Lemma
8.5.6
characterizes a
\theta
-morphism by formal functions. When operator gen-semigroups are induced from formal functions, the criterion for a
\theta
-morphism by Lemma
8.5.6
is usually more convenient than that by Definition
8.5.4
. Thus Lemma
8.5.6
simplifies our proofs of the following several results.

The following generalizes both Propositions
6.1.10
and
8.3.4
.

Proposition 8.5.7
.

Let
F/B

(
resp.
{F}^{\prime}/{B}^{\prime}
)
be a field extension, let an operator gen-semigroup
T

(
resp.
{T}^{\prime}
)
be defined as in Example
8.1.3
on
F

(
resp.
{F}^{\prime}
)
over
B

(
resp.
{B}^{\prime}
)
, let
\varphi:B\to{B}^{\prime}
be a field isomorphism, and let

\vartheta:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]}\to\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{{B}^{\prime}[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]}

be the map given by
f\mapsto{f}^{\prime}
where

f({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}})=\sum{{c_{p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}}}x_{1}^{{{p}_{1}}}\cdots x_{n}^{{{p}_{n}}}}

and

{f}^{\prime}({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}})=\sum{\varphi({c_{p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}}})x_{1}^{{{p}_{1}}}\cdots x_{n}^{{{p}_{n}}}}.

Let
\theta=\{(\tau(f),{\tau}^{\prime}(\vartheta(f)))\,|\,f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}],n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
, where
\tau
and
{\tau}^{\prime}
are the maps inducing
T
and
{T}^{\prime}
, respectively, defined as in Example
8.1.3
.

Then
\forall(\emptyset\neq)U\subseteq F
and
(\emptyset\neq)V\subseteq{F}^{\prime}
, a map
\phi:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}^{\prime}}}}
is a ring homomorphism extending
\varphi
if and only if
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism.

Proof.

We may prove Proposition
8.5.7
in a way analogous to the one for Proposition
6.1.10
. However, now we employ Lemma
8.5.6
instead.

By Example
8.1.3
,

T=\{f^{*}:{{F}^{n}}\to F
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}],n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}

and

{T}^{\prime}=\{f^{*}:{{{F}^{\prime}}^{n}}\to{F}^{\prime}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in{B}^{\prime}[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}],n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Thus
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}^{\prime}}}}
are the rings
B[U]
and
{B}^{\prime}[V]
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{{B}^{\prime}[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]}
. Then Lemma
8.5.6
applies. Hence it suffices to show that
\phi:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{{{T}^{\prime}}}}
is a ring homomorphism extending
\varphi
if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.5.6
is true in this case. Specifically, we only need to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall c\in B
,
\phi(c)=\varphi(c)
, and
\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
,
\phi(a+b)=\phi(a)+\phi(b)
and
\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle U\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
and
f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
,

(8.5)

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=\vartheta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2):
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}})=\sum{{c_{p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}}}x_{1}^{{{p}_{1}}}\cdots x_{n}^{{{p}_{n}}}}\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle U\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
,

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\phi(\sum{{c_{p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}}}a_{1}^{{{p}_{1}}}\cdots a_{n}^{{{p}_{n}}}})

=\sum{\phi({c_{p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}}})(\phi(a_{1}))^{p_{1}}\cdots(\phi(a_{n}))^{p_{n}}}
(because statement (1) is true)

=\sum{\varphi({c_{p_{1},\cdots,p_{n}}})(\phi(a_{1}))^{p_{1}}\cdots(\phi(a_{n}))^{p_{n}}}
(because
\forall c\in B
,
\phi(c)=\varphi(c)
)

=\vartheta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))
(by the definition of
\vartheta
).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Considering the case where
f\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
is a constant polynomial, we can tell from Equation
(
8.5
)
and the definition of
\vartheta
that
\forall c\in B
,
\phi(c)=\varphi(c)
.

Let
a,b\in{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
. Let
g={{x}_{1}}+{{x}_{2}}
and let
h={{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}}
. Then

\phi(a+b)

=\phi(g(a,b))

=\vartheta(g)(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(by Equation
(
8.5
)
)

=g(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(
\vartheta(g)=g
by the definition of
\vartheta
)

=\phi(a)+\phi(b)
.

And

\phi(ab)

=\phi(h(a,b))

=\vartheta(h)(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(by Equation
(
8.5
)
)

=h(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(
\vartheta(h)=h
by the definition of
\vartheta
)

=\phi(a)\phi(b)
. ∎

The following is for ring homomorphisms between not necessarily commutative rings.

Proposition 8.5.8
.

Let
A
and
R
be rings with identity, let
{{T}_{A}}

(
resp.
{{T}_{R}}
)
be the operator gen-semigroup on
A

(
resp.
R
)
defined as in Example
8.1.4
, and let
\theta=\{({{\tau}_{A}}(f),{{\tau}_{R}}(f))\,|\,f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
, where
{{\tau}_{A}}
and
{{\tau}_{R}}
are the maps inducing
{{T}_{A}}
and
{{T}_{R}}
, respectively, defined as in Example
8.1.4
. Then a map
\phi:A\to R
is a ring homomorphism if and only if
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism.

Proof.

By Example
8.1.4
,

{{T}_{A}}=\{f^{*}:{{A}^{n}}\to A
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
,

and

{{T}_{R}}=\{f^{*}:{{R}^{n}}\to R
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Obviously
{{\left\langle A\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{A}}}}=A
and
{{\left\langle R\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{R}}}}=R
. Hence
\phi:A\to R
is
\phi:{{\left\langle A\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{A}}}}\to{{\left\langle R\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{R}}}}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}={\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle}
and let
\vartheta=\operatorname{Id}
. Then Lemma
8.5.6
applies. By Lemma
8.5.6
, we only need to show that
\phi:{{\left\langle A\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{A}}}}\to{{\left\langle R\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{R}}}}
is a ring homomorphism if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.5.6
is true in this case. Specifically, it suffices to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\phi({{1}_{A}})={{1}_{R}}
and
\forall a,b\in A
,
\phi(a+b)=\phi(a)+\phi(b)
and
\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{A}^{n}}
and
f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle
,

(8.6)

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Since
f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle
is a polynomial, it is not hard to see that statement (2) is true.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Considering the case where
f\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}\right\rangle
is the constant polynomial 1, we can tell from Equation
(
8.6
)
that
\phi({{1}_{A}})={{1}_{R}}
.

Let
a,b\in A
. Let
g={{x}_{1}}+{{x}_{2}}
and
h={{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}}
. Then

\phi(a+b)

=\phi(g(a,b))

=g(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(by Equation
(
8.6
)
)

=\phi(a)+\phi(b)
.

And

\phi(ab)

=\phi(h(a,b))

=h(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(by Equation
(
8.6
)
)

=\phi(a)\phi(b)
.
∎

Proposition
8.3.5
is generalized to

Proposition 8.5.9
.

Let
M
and
N
be modules over a commutative ring
R
with identity, let
{{T}_{M}}

(
resp.
{{T}_{N}}
)
be the operator gen-semigroup on
M

(
resp.
N
)
defined as in Example
8.1.5
, and let

\theta=\{({{\tau}_{M}}(f),{{\tau}_{N}}(f))\,|\,f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\},

where
{{\tau}_{M}}
and
{{\tau}_{N}}
are the maps inducing
{{T}_{M}}
and
{{T}_{N}}
, respectively, defined as in Example
8.1.5
.

If
X
and
Y
are nonempty subsets of
M
and
N
, respectively, then a map
\phi:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{M}}}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{N}}}}
is an R-homomorphism of R-modules if and only if
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism.

Proof.

Our proof is comparable with that of Proposition
8.3.5
.

By Example
8.1.5
,

{{T}_{M}}=\{f^{*}:{{M}^{n}}\to M
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}

and

{{T}_{N}}=\{f^{*}:{{N}^{n}}\to N
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Clearly,
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{M}}}}
and
{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{N}}}}
are the submodules of
M
and
N
generated by
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{F}={\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}}
and let
\vartheta=\operatorname{Id}
. Then Lemma
8.5.6
applies. By Lemma
8.5.6
, it suffices to show that
\phi:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{M}}}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{N}}}}
is an
R
-homomorphism of
R
-modules if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.5.6
is true in this case. Specifically, we only need to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{M}}}}
and
r\in R
,
\phi(a+b)=\phi(a)+\phi(b)
and
\phi(ra)=r\phi(a)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle X\right\rangle_{{{T}_{M}}}^{n}
and
f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}
,

(8.7)

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Because
f\in\{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{{{r}_{i}}{{x}_{i}}}\,|\,{{r}_{i}}\in R\}
is a linear polynomial whose constant term is zero, it is not hard to see that statement (2) is true.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1): Let
a,b\in{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{M}}}}
and let
r\in R
. Let
g={{x}_{1}}+{{x}_{2}}
and let
h=r{{x}_{1}}
. Then

\phi(a+b)

=\phi(g(a,b))

=g(\phi(a),\phi(b))
(by Equation
(
8.7
)
)

=\phi(a)+\phi(b)
.

And

\phi(ra)

=\phi(h(a))

=h(\phi(a))
(by Equation
(
8.7
)
)

=r\phi(a)
.
∎

Proposition
8.3.6
is generalized as follows.

Proposition 8.5.10
.

Let
G
and
H
be abelian groups, let
{{T}_{G}}

(
resp.
{{T}_{H}}
)
be the operator gen-semigroup on
G

(
resp.
H
)
defined as in Example
8.1.6
, and let
\theta=\{({{\tau}_{G}}(f),{{\tau}_{H}}(f))\,|\,f\in\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
, where
{{\tau}_{G}}
and
{{\tau}_{H}}
are the maps inducing
{{T}_{G}}
and
{{T}_{H}}
, respectively, defined as in Example
8.1.6
.

If
X
and
Y
are nonempty subsets of
G
and
H
respectively, then a map
\phi:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{H}}}}
is a group homomorphism if and only if
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism.

Proof.

By Example
8.1.6
,

{{T}_{G}}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{G}^{n}}\to G}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
}

and

{{T}_{H}}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{H}^{n}}\to H}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
}.

Besides,
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}
and
{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{H}}}}
are the subgroups of
G
and
H
generated by
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{F}={\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}}
and let
\vartheta=\operatorname{Id}
. Then Lemma
8.5.6
applies. By Lemma
8.5.6
, it suffices to show that
\phi:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{H}}}}
is a group homomorphism if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.5.6
is true in this case. Specifically, we only need to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}
,
\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle X\right\rangle_{{{T}_{G}}}^{n}
and
f\in\{\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}{x_{i}^{{{p}_{i}}}}\,|\,{{p}_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})).

It is not hard to see (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2). ∎

Proposition
8.3.7
is generalized to

Proposition 8.5.11
.

Let
G
and
H
be groups, let
{{T}_{G}}

(
resp.
{{T}_{H}}
)
be the operator gen-semigroup on
G

(
resp.
H
)
defined as in Example
8.1.7
, and let
\theta=\{({{\tau}_{G}}(f),{{\tau}_{H}}(f))\,|\,f\in\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
, where
{{\tau}_{G}}
and
{{\tau}_{H}}
are the maps inducing
{{T}_{G}}
and
{{T}_{H}}
, respectively, defined as in Example
8.1.7
.

If
X
and
Y
are nonempty subsets of
G
and
H
, respectively, then a map
\phi:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{H}}}}
is a group homomorphism if and only if
\phi
is a
\theta
-morphism.

Proof.

Our proof is comparable with that of Proposition
8.5.10
.

By Example
8.1.7
,

T=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{G}^{n}}\to G}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle\}
.

and

T=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\{f^{*}:{{H}^{n}}\to H}
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle\}
.

Clearly,
{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}
and
{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{H}}}}
are the subgroups of
G
and
H
generated by
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{F}={\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}=\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle}
and let
\vartheta=\operatorname{Id}
. Then Lemma
8.5.6
applies. By Lemma
8.5.6
, we only need to show that
\phi:{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}\to{{\left\langle Y\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{H}}}}
is a group homomorphism if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
8.5.6
is true in this case. Specifically, it suffices to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle X\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{G}}}}
,
\phi(ab)=\phi(a)\phi(b)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle X\right\rangle_{{{T}_{G}}}^{n}
and
f\in\left\langle{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}},x_{1}^{-1},\cdots,x_{n}^{-1}\right\rangle
,

\phi(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}})).

It is not hard to see (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2).
∎

8.6.
\theta
-isomorphisms

For
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
being operator gen-semigroups, Definition
6.2.1
still applies: a
\theta
-morphism from a
T_{1}
-space
S_{1}
to a
T_{2}
-space is a
\theta
-isomorphism
if it is bijective. To justify the definition, we need to show Lemma
6.2.2
and Proposition
6.2.3
for the case of operator gen-semigroups as follows.

Lemma 8.6.1
.

(Lemma
6.2.2
)

Let
\phi
be a
\theta
-isomorphism from a
T_{1}
-space
S_{1}
to a
T_{2}
-space
S_{2}
. Then
\theta^{-1}{{|}_{S_{1}}}
is a map, where
\theta^{-1}:=\{(g,f)\,|\,(f,g)\in\theta\}
.

Proof.

Let
(g,f_{1}),(g,f_{2})\in\theta^{-1}
with
g
of
n
variables. Then by Corollary
8.5.5
, both
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
have
n
variables.
Let
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in S_{1}^{n}
.
Then to show that
\theta^{-1}{{|}_{S_{1}}}
is a map, by Proposition
8.5.3
and Definition
8.5.2
, it suffices to show that

f_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=f_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
. Moreover, since
\phi
is injective, we only need to show
\phi(f_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=\phi(f_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
:

\phi(f_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\theta(f_{1})(\phi(a_{1}),\cdots,\phi(a_{n}))
(by Definition
8.5.4
)

=g(\phi(a_{1}),\cdots,\phi(a_{n}))
(because
(f_{1},g)\in\theta
and
\theta{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}
is a map by Corollary
8.5.5
)

=\theta(f_{2})(\phi(a_{1}),\cdots,\phi(a_{n}))
(because
(f_{2},g)\in\theta
and
\theta{|}_{\operatorname{Im}\phi}
is a map)

=\phi(f_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
(by Definition
8.5.4
).
∎

Proposition 8.6.2
.

(Proposition
6.2.3
)

Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be a
T_{1}
-space and a
T_{2}
-space, respectively, let
\phi\in\operatorname{Iso}_{\theta}({{S}_{1}},{{S}_{2}})
and let
{{\phi}^{-1}}
be the inverse map of
\phi
. Then
{{\phi}^{-1}}\in\operatorname{Iso}_{\theta^{-1}}({{S}_{2}},{{S}_{1}})
.

Proof.

By Definition
8.5.4
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{S_{1}^{n}}
and
f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
of
n
variables,

f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=\phi^{-1}(\theta(f)(\phi({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\phi({{a}_{n}}))).

Hence
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}},f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
of
n
variables and
(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})\in{S_{2}^{n}}
,

(8.8)

f({\phi}^{-1}({b}_{1}),\cdots,{\phi}^{-1}({b}_{n}))=\phi^{-1}(\theta(f)(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})).

By Lemma
8.6.1
,
\theta^{-1}{{|}_{S_{1}}}
is a map. Then by Proposition
8.5.3
,
\forall g\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta^{-1}(=\operatorname{Im}\theta)
,
\exists n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
such that
\forall(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in S_{1}^{n}
,
\theta^{-1}(g)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined, and from (
8.8
), we can tell that the number of variables of
g
is also
n
.

Thus
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
g\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta^{-1}
of
n
variables and
(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})\in{S_{2}^{n}}
, it follows from (
8.8
) that

\theta^{-1}(g)({\phi}^{-1}({b}_{1}),\cdots,{\phi}^{-1}({b}_{n}))={{\phi}^{-1}}(g(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})).

Hence by Definition
8.5.4
,
\phi^{-1}
is a
\theta^{-1}
-morphism from
S_{2}
to
S_{1}
.

Moreover, since
\phi^{-1}
is bijective, by Definition
6.2.1
,
{{\phi}^{-1}}\in\operatorname{Iso}_{\theta^{-1}}({{S}_{2}},{{S}_{1}})
.
∎

For operator gen-semigroups, we will generalize most results obtained in Sections
2
to
7
. Before doing this in Sections
10
to
14
, however, we shall generalize the notion of operator gen-semigroup further in the next section.

9.
Basic notions for the case of (multivariable) partial functions

In this section, we shall allow elements in
T
to be partial functions. Then we will find that some more familiar concepts such as ring homomorphisms between fields and covariant functors between categories can be characterized in terms of
T
-morphisms or
\theta
-morphisms.

Subsection
9.1
generalizes the notion of operator gen-semigroup. Then correspondingly, the notions of
T
-spaces,
T
-morphisms,
T
-isomorphisms,
\theta
-morphisms and
\theta
-isomorphisms are generalized in Sections
9.2
,
9.3
,
9.4
,
9.5
and
9.6
, respectively.

9.1.
Partial-operator generalized-semigroup
T

Recall the concept of partial functions as follows.

Definition 9.1.1
.

Let
X
and
Y
be sets. A
partial function

f
of
n(\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}})
variables from the cartesian product
{X}^{n}
to
Y
is a (total) function from a subset
S
of
{X}^{n}
to
Y
, and
S
, which may be empty, is called the
domain of definition
of
f
.

Moreover, let
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})\in X^{m}
, where
m\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
. If
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})
lies in the domain of definition of
f
, then
f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})
is said to be
well-defined
.

Remark
.

By the definition, any function is a partial function.

We are going to extend our study to partial functions. First, we generalize Definition
8.1.1
to

Definition 9.1.2
.

Let
D
be a set, let
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
, and let
f
be a partial function from the cartesian product
{{D}^{n}}
to
D
.
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
, let
n_{i}\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
and let
{{g}_{i}}
be a partial function from the cartesian product
{{D}^{{{n}_{i}}}}
to
D
. Let
m=\sum\nolimits_{i}{{n_{i}}}
. Then the
composite

f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})
is the partial function from
{{D}^{m}}
to
D
given by

({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{m}})\mapsto f({{g}_{1}}({{\text{v}}_{1}}),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}({{\text{v}}_{n}})),

where
{{\text{v}}_{i}}:=({{x}_{{{m}_{i}}+1}},\cdots,{{x}_{{{m}_{i}}+{{n}_{i}}}})\in{{D}^{{{n}_{i}}}},\forall i=1,\cdots,n
,
{{m}_{1}}:=0
and
{{m}_{i}}:=\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{i-1}{{{n}_{k}}},\forall i=2,\cdots,n
.

Remark
.

(1)

Note that
({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{m}})
is in the domain of definition of
f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})
if and only if each
{{g}_{i}}({{\text{v}}_{i}})
is well-defined and
f({{g}_{1}}({{\text{v}}_{1}}),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}({{\text{v}}_{n}}))
is well-defined.

(2)

We may denote the
n
-tuple
(g,g,\cdots,g)
by
{{g}^{n}}
(in e.g. Section
14
) for brevity.

Before we generalize Definition
8.1.2
, we need a terminology as follows.

Terminology 9.1.3
.

Let
f
and
g
be partial functions from
A
to
B
. If the domain of definition of
f
contains the domain of definition of
g
, which is denoted by
D(g)
, and
f{{|}_{D(g)}}=g{{|}_{D(g)}}
, then we say that
g
is a
restriction
of
f
.

Remark
.

Trivially, if
D(g)=\emptyset
, then
g
is a restriction of any
f
.

Definition 9.1.4
.

Let
D
be a set and let
T
be a subset of

{all partial functions from the cartesian product
{{D}^{n}}
to
D\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
}.

If
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
of
n
variables, and
({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})\in{{T}^{n}}
(cartesian product), the composite
f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})
is a restriction of some element of
T
, then we call
T
a
partial-operator generalized-semigroup
, abbreviated as
par-operator gen-semigroup
, on
D
and
D
is called the
domain
of
T
.

Remark
.

(1)

Trivially,
\emptyset
is a par-operator gen-semigroup.

(2)

Obviously, any operator gen-semigroup is a par-operator gen-semigroup.

Definition
2.1.6
is generalized to

Definition 9.1.5
.

Let
D
be a set and
G
be a subset of

{all partial functions from the cartesian product
{{D}^{n}}
to
D\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
}.

Then we call the intersection of all par-operator gen-semigroups on
D
containing
G
the par-operator gen-semigroup on
D

generated by

G
, and denote it by
\left\langle G\right\rangle
.

Remark
.

From Definition
9.1.4
, we can tell that
\left\langle G\right\rangle
is the smallest par-operator gen-semigroup on
D
which contains
G
.

Example 9.1.6
.

Let
B
be a subfield of a field
F
. Let

T=\{
the partial function
f^{*}:{{F}^{n}}\to F
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\text{Frac}(B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]),n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Then it is not hard to see that
T
is a par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
. The map which induces
T
, i.e.
\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{\text{Frac}(B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}])}\to T
given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
, is surjective, but it is not necessarily injective.

Example 9.1.7
.

Let
B
be a differential subfield of a differential field
F
; that is, field
F
is endowed with a derivation and the derivation of
F
restricts to the derivation of
B
. Let
T=\left\langle{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}\right\rangle
(Definition
9.1.5
), where
{{T}_{F}}
is the par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined as in Example
9.1.6
and
{{T}_{\partial}}
is the operator semigroup defined in Example
2.1.5
. Then by Definition
9.1.5
,
T
is a par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
.

Example 9.1.8
.

For this example,
D
in Definitions
9.1.2
and
9.1.4
is generalized to be a class. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category and let
D
be the collection of all morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
, let

{{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}=\{
word
w
on
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}\,|
each of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
arises in
w
}.

Let
T=\{
the partial function
f^{*}:{{D}^{n}}\to D
given by

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in{{\mathcal{F}}_{n}},n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
,

where
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is defined to be the composite
{{a}_{{{i}_{1}}}}\circ\cdots\circ{{a}_{{{i}_{k}}}}
for
f={{x}_{{{i}_{1}}}}\cdots{{x}_{{{i}_{k}}}}
(where
{{i}_{1}},\cdots,{{i}_{k}}\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
).

In particular, for
f=x_{1}\in{{\mathcal{F}}_{1}}
,
f^{*}
is the identity function on
D
.

Thus, for
f\in{{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in D^{n}
and
{{i}_{1}},\cdots,{{i}_{k}}\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, the following four statements are equivalent:

(i)

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is in the domain of definition of the
f^{*}
induced by
f={{x}_{{{i}_{1}}}}\cdots{{x}_{{{i}_{k}}}}
.

(ii)

for
f={{x}_{{{i}_{1}}}}\cdots{{x}_{{{i}_{k}}}}
,
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined.

(iii)

{{a}_{{{i}_{1}}}}\circ\cdots\circ{{a}_{{{i}_{k}}}}
is well-defined.

(iv)

\forall j=1,\cdots,(k-1)
, the domain (object) of
{{a}_{{{i}_{j}}}}
is the target (object) of
{{a}_{{{i}_{j+1}}}}
.

Then it is not hard to see that
T
is a par-operator gen-semigroup on
D
. Let
\tau:\bigcup\nolimits_{n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}}{{{\mathcal{F}}_{n}}}\to T
be the map inducing
T
given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
.

9.2.
T
-spaces

Definition 9.2.1
.

Let
T
be a par-operator gen-semigroup on
D
and let
U\subseteq D
.

Then we call

S:=\{
well-defined
f({{u}_{1}},\cdots,{{u}_{n}})\,|\,f\in T
,
({{u}_{1}},\cdots,{{u}_{n}})\in{{U}^{n}}
,
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}

the
T
-space
generated by
U
and denote
S
by
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
.

Moreover, if
B\subseteq S
is also a
T
-space, then we say that
B
is a
T
-subspace
of
S
and write
B\leq S
or
S\geq B
.

Example 9.2.2
.

Let
B
be a subfield of a field
F
. Let
T
be the par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined in Example
9.1.6
. Then
\forall(\emptyset\neq)U\subseteq F
, we can tell that the
T
-space
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
is the field
B(U)
.

Example 9.2.3
.

Let
B
be a differential subfield of a differential field
F
. Let
T(=\left\langle{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}\right\rangle)
be the par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined in Example
9.1.7
. Then we claim that
\forall(\emptyset\neq)U\subseteq F
, the
T
-space
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
is the differential subfield of
F
generated by
U
over
B
.

To show the claim, let
K={{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
. Then by Proposition
10.1.1
(to be shown in Section
10
), which generalizes Proposition
2.2.7
,
{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{T}}\subseteq K
. And
{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{F}}}}\subseteq{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{T}}
because
{{T}_{F}}\subseteq T
. Hence
{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{F}}}}\subseteq K
. On the other hand,
{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{F}}}}\supseteq K
because
\operatorname{Id}\in{{T}_{F}}
. Therefore
{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{F}}}}=K
, and thus by Example
9.2.2
,
K
is a field. Analogously, we can show
{{\left\langle K\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{\partial}}}}=K
, and hence
K
is a differential field.

Example 9.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. Let
D
and
T
be defined as in Example
9.1.8
. Then the
T
-space
{{\left\langle D\right\rangle}_{T}}=D
since
\operatorname{Id}\in T
. Because there is a bijection between the objects
A
in
\mathcal{C}
and their identity morphisms
1_{A}
, we may view
{{\left\langle D\right\rangle}_{T}}
as
\mathcal{C}
.

9.3.
T
-morphisms

Now we generalize Definition
8.3.1
to

Definition 9.3.1
.

Let
T
be a par-operator gen-semigroup. Let
\sigma
be a map from a
T
-space
S
to a
T
-space. If
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in T
, neither
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,

\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})),

then we call
\sigma
a
T
-morphism
.

Remark
.

From Definitions
9.1.4
and
9.2.1
(or from Proposition
10.1.1
), we can tell that if
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined, then
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in S
, and so
\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
is well-defined.

Note that in Examples
9.1.6
and
9.1.8
, par-operator gen-semigroups are induced from rational functions or words on a set. Hence we generalize Definition
8.3.2
as follows.

Definition 9.3.2
.

Let
f
be an expression associated with a rule
R
. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

If all of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
arise as symbols in
f
, and there are sets
C
and
D
such that, by the rule
R
,
f
yields nothing or a single element in
C
, which we denote by
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})(\in C)
, whenever every
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
in
f
is replaced by any
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in D
, respectively, then we call the couple
(f,R)
a (
C
-valued)
formal partial function
of
n
variables
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
and
D
is called a
domain
of
(f,R)
.

Moreover, in the case where
a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\in D
and
f
yields
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
, we say that
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is
well-defined
or
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is in the
domain of definition
of
f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
.

Besides, we may call
f
a
formal partial function
if its associated rule
R
is clear from the context.

Remark
.

Analogous to a formal function, a formal partial function may have more than one domain. And the notion of formal partial function generalizes the notion of partial function.

When par-operator gen-semigroups are induced from formal partial functions, the following lemma, which generalizes Lemma
8.3.3
, will be useful.

Lemma 9.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a set of
D
-valued formal partial functions on a domain
D
and let
T
be a par-operator gen-semigroup on
D
induced by
\mathcal{F}
; that is, there is a surjective map
\tau:\mathcal{F}\to T
being given by
f\mapsto f^{*}
, where
f^{*}:{{D}^{n}}\to D
is given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
for
f
of
n
variables,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
. Let
\sigma
be a map from a
T
-space
S
to a
T
-space. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\sigma
is a
T
-morphism.

(ii)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
of
n
variables, neither
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

(9.1)

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): By Definition
9.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}
of
n
variables, neither
\tau(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
\tau(f)(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or
\sigma(\tau(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=\tau(f)(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
.

Then by the definition of
\tau
, (ii) must be true.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i):
Since
\tau
is surjective,
\forall f^{*}\in T
,
\exists f\in\mathcal{F}
such that
f^{*}=\tau(f)
. Then from statement (ii) and the definition of
\tau
, we can tell that
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
and
f^{*}\in T
of
n
variables, neither
f^{*}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f^{*}(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma(f^{*}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f^{*}(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
.

So by Definition
9.3.1
,
\sigma
must be a
T
-morphism.
∎

Lemma
9.3.3
characterizes a
T
-morphism by formal partial functions which induce
T
. When a par-operator gen-semigroup is induced from formal partial functions, the criterion for a
T
-morphism by Lemma
9.3.3
is normally more convenient than that by Definition
9.3.1
.

The following is comparable with Proposition
8.3.4
, except that now
T
is defined by Example
9.1.6
, and hence
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
must be fields.

Proposition 9.3.4
.

Let
B
be a subfield of a field
F
, let
T
be the par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined in Example
9.1.6
, and let nonempty
U,V\subseteq F
. Then a map
\sigma:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely if and only if it is a
T
-morphism.

Proof.

By the definition of
T
in Example
9.1.6
,

T=\{
the partial function
f^{*}:{{F}^{n}}\to F
given by
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\mapsto f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\,|\,f\in\text{Frac}(B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]),n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
.

Then
{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
are the fields
B(U)
and
B(V)
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{F}=\{f\in\text{Frac}(B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}])\ |\ n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}\}
. Then Lemma
9.3.3
applies. Hence it suffices to show that
\sigma:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely if and only if statement (ii) in Lemma
9.3.3
is true in this case. Specifically, we only need to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\forall c\in B
,
\sigma(c)=c
, and
\forall a,b\in{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}(=B(U))
,

\sigma(a+b)=\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)
and
\sigma(ab)=\sigma(a)\sigma(b)
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle U\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
and
f\in\text{Frac}(B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}])
, neither

f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

(9.2)

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2): Let
f({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}})=g({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}})/h({{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}})
, where
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
and
h,g\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
. Let
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\left\langle U\right\rangle_{T}^{n}
. Then

f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined;

\Leftrightarrow h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\neq 0
;

\Leftrightarrow\sigma(h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))\neq 0
(by statement (1));

\Leftrightarrow f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))

=g(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))/h(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))

=\sigma(g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))/\sigma(h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
(because
h,g\in B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}]
and statement (1) is true)

is well-defined.

Moreover, in the case where any of the above equivalent conditions is true,

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\sigma(g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})/h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))

=\sigma(g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))/\sigma(h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))
.

Combining the above four equations, we have

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1):
Considering the case where
f\in\text{Frac}(B[{{x}_{1}},\cdots,{{x}_{n}}])
is a constant polynomial, we can tell from statement (2) that
\forall c\in B
,
\sigma(c)=c
.

Let
a,b\in{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
. Let
g={{x}_{1}}+{{x}_{2}}
and
h={{x}_{1}}{{x}_{2}}
.

Then

\sigma(a+b)

=\sigma(g(a,b))

=g(\sigma(a),\sigma(b))
(by Equation
(
9.2
)
)

=\sigma(a)+\sigma(b)
.

And

\sigma(ab)

=\sigma(h(a,b))

=h(\sigma(a),\sigma(b))
(by Equation
(
9.2
)
)

=\sigma(a)\sigma(b)
.
∎

However,
\sigma
in Proposition
9.3.4
is between field extensions over the same field
B
. We will deal with ring homomorphisms between field extensions over different fields in Subsection
9.5
.

To characterize differential ring homomorphisms between differential fields by
T
-morphisms, we need a lemma as follows.

Lemma 9.3.5
.

Let
T
be a par-operator gen-semigroup, let
\sigma
be a map from a
T
-space
S
to a
T
-space, let
f\in T
have
n
variables, and
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
, let
{{g}_{i}}\in T
have
{{n}_{i}}
variables. Suppose that both of the following two conditions are satisfied,

(i)

\forall(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{S}^{n}}
, neither
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or
\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
;

(ii)

\forall i=1,\cdots,n
and
({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{{{n}_{i}}}})\in{{S}^{{{n}_{i}}}}
,

neither
{{g}_{i}}({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{{{n}_{i}}}})
nor
{{g}_{i}}(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{{{n}_{i}}}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma({{g}_{i}}({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{{{n}_{i}}}}))={{g}_{i}}(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{{{n}_{i}}}})).

Then
\forall({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})\in{{S}^{m}}
, where
m=\sum\nolimits_{i}{{{n}_{i}}}
,

neither
(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})
nor
(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{m}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma((f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}}))=(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{m}})).

Proof.

Let
({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})\in{{S}^{m}}
, where
m=\sum\nolimits_{i}{{{n}_{i}}}
. Then

(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})
is well-defined;

\Leftrightarrow f({{g}_{1}}({{\text{u}}_{1}}),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}({{\text{u}}_{n}}))
is well-defined, where
{{\text{u}}_{i}}=({{a}_{{{m}_{i}}+1}},\cdots,{{a}_{{{m}_{i}}+{{n}_{i}}}})
,
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
,
{{m}_{1}}=0
, and
{{m}_{i}}=\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{i-1}{{{n}_{k}}},\forall i=2,\cdots,n
; (by Definition
9.1.2
)

\Leftrightarrow f(\sigma({{g}_{1}}({{\text{u}}_{1}})),\cdots,\sigma({{g}_{n}}({{\text{u}}_{n}})))
is well-defined; (by condition (i) and the fact that each
{{g}_{i}}({{\text{u}}_{i}})\in S
(
cf.
Proposition
10.1.1
in Section
10
))

\Leftrightarrow f({{g}_{1}}(\sigma({{\text{u}}_{1}})),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}(\sigma({{\text{u}}_{n}})))
is well-defined, where
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
,
\sigma\text{(}{{\text{u}}_{i}}):=(\sigma\text{(}{{a}_{{{m}_{i}}+1}}),\cdots,\sigma\text{(}{{a}_{{{m}_{i}}+{{n}_{i}}}}))
; (by condition (ii))

\Leftrightarrow(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{m}}))
is well-defined. (by Definition
9.1.2
)

And in the case where any of the above five equivalent conditions is true,

(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})\in S
(by Proposition
10.1.1
)

and

\sigma((f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}}))

=\sigma(f({{g}_{1}}({{\text{u}}_{1}}),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}({{\text{u}}_{n}})))
(by Definition
9.1.2
)

=f(\sigma({{g}_{1}}({{\text{u}}_{1}})),\cdots,\sigma({{g}_{n}}({{\text{u}}_{n}})))
(by (i) and the fact that each
{{g}_{i}}({{\text{u}}_{i}})\in S
(by Proposition
10.1.1
))

=f({{g}_{1}}(\sigma({{\text{u}}_{1}})),\cdots,{{g}_{n}}(\sigma({{\text{u}}_{n}})))
(by condition (ii))

=(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{m}}))
(by Definition
9.1.2
)
∎

Proposition 9.3.6
.

Let
B
be a differential subfield of a differential field
F
, let
T
be the par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined in Example
9.1.7
, and let nonempty
U,V\subseteq F
. Then a map
\sigma:{{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}\to{{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
is a differential ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely if and only if it is a
T
-morphism.

Proof.

Let
A={{\left\langle U\right\rangle}_{T}}
and
R={{\left\langle V\right\rangle}_{T}}
. By Example
9.2.3
,
A
and
R
are the differential subfields of
F
generated by
U
and
V
over
B
, respectively.

Recall that a ring homomorphism
\sigma:A\to R
is a differential homomorphism if and only if
\sigma
commutes with
\partial
. Then by Definition
9.3.1
, we only need to show that the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\sigma
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely and
\forall a\in A
,
\sigma(\partial(a))=\partial(\sigma(a))
.

(2)

\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{A}^{n}}
and
f\in T
of
n
variables, neither
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma(f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2):
Let
{{T}_{F}}
be the par-operator gen-semigroup on
F
defined as in Example
9.1.6
and let
{{T}_{\partial}}
be the operator semigroup defined in Example
2.1.5
.

Since
\sigma
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely, by Proposition
9.3.4
,
\sigma
is a
{{T}_{F}}
-morphism. By Definition
9.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{A}^{n}}
, and
g\in{{T}_{F}}
of
n
variables, neither
g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
g(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or
\sigma(g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=g(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
. Moreover, since
\sigma(\partial(a))=\partial(\sigma(a)),\forall a\in A
, we can tell that
\forall g\in{{T}_{\partial}}=\{{{\partial}^{n}}\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\}
and
a\in A
,
\sigma(g(a))=g(\sigma(a))
.

Therefore,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{A}^{n}}
, and
g\in{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}
of
n
variables, neither
g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
g(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or
\sigma(g(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=g(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
.

Let
f\in{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}
have
n
variables, let
{{g}_{i}}\in{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}
have
{{n}_{i}}
variables,
\forall i=1,\cdots,n
, and let
({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})\in{{A}^{m}}
, where
m=\sum\nolimits_{i}{{{n}_{i}}}
. By the statement in the preceding paragraph, Lemma
9.3.5
applies (with
T:=\left\langle{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}\right\rangle
). It follows that neither
(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}})
nor
(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{m}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma((f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))({{a}_{1}},\cdots,{{a}_{m}}))=(f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}}))(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{m}})).

Moreover, since
T=\left\langle{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}\right\rangle
, from Definitions
9.1.4
and
9.1.5
, we can tell that the above
f\circ({{g}_{1}},\cdots,{{g}_{n}})
is a restriction of some element of
T
, and
\forall h\in T
,
h
can be obtained by a finite number of compositions (defined by Definition
9.1.2
) of elements of
{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}
(or elements which “originally come from”
{{T}_{F}}\bigcup{{T}_{\partial}}
). Thus, by induction and application of Lemma
9.3.5
as in the preceding paragraph, we can show that
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
h\in T
of
n
variables and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{{A}^{n}}
, neither
h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
h(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

\sigma(h(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}))=h(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}})).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1):
As shown in Example
9.2.3
,
{{\left\langle A\right\rangle}_{{{T}_{F}}}}=A
. Thus
A
is also a
{{T}_{F}}
-space. Then because
{{T}_{F}}\subseteq T
, by Definition
9.3.1
,
\sigma
is a
{{T}_{F}}
-morphism. Hence by Proposition
9.3.4
,
\sigma
is a ring homomorphism with
B
fixed pointwisely. Moreover, if let
f=\partial(\in T)
, then by statement (2),
\sigma(\partial(a))=\partial(\sigma(a)),\forall a\in A
. ∎

9.4.
T
-isomorphisms

To par-operator gen-semigroups, Definition
2.4.1
still applies: if a
T
-morphism is bijective, then we call it a
T
-isomorphism
. To justify the definition, we need to show Proposition
2.4.2
with a different proof as follows.

Proposition 9.4.1
.

(Proposition
2.4.2
)

Let
\sigma
be a
T
-isomorphism from a
T
-space
{{S}_{1}}
to a
T
-space
{{S}_{2}}
and let
{{\sigma}^{-1}}
be the inverse map of
\sigma
. Then
{{\sigma}^{-1}}\in\operatorname{Iso}_{T}({{S}_{2}},{{S}_{1}})
.

Proof.

By Definition
9.3.1
,
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
of
n
variables and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in{S_{1}^{n}}
, neither
f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,

\sigma({{a}_{n}}))
is well-defined or

f(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})={\sigma}^{-1}(f(\sigma({{a}_{1}}),\cdots,\sigma({{a}_{n}}))).

Hence
\forall n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
,
f\in T
of
n
variables and
(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})\in{S_{2}^{n}}
, neither
f({b}_{1},\cdots,{b}_{n})
nor
f({\sigma}^{-1}({b}_{1}),\cdots,{\sigma}^{-1}({b}_{n}))
is well-defined or

f({\sigma}^{-1}({b}_{1}),\cdots,{\sigma}^{-1}({b}_{n}))={\sigma}^{-1}(f(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})).

Then by Definition
9.3.1
,
{\sigma}^{-1}
is a
T
-morphism from
S_{2}
to
S_{1}
, and hence
{{\sigma}^{-1}}\in\operatorname{Iso}_{T}({{S}_{2}},{{S}_{1}})
by Definition
2.4.1
.
∎

9.5.
\theta
-morphisms

We first generalize Notation
8.5.1
to

Notation 9.5.1
.

Let
D_{1}
(resp.
D_{2}
) be a set, let
M_{1}
(resp.
M_{2}
) be a subset of
{all partial functions from the cartesian product
D_{1}^{n}
to
{D_{1}}\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
}
(resp. a subset of {all partial functions from
D_{2}^{n}
to
{{D}_{2}}\,|\,n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
}),
let
\theta\subseteq{{M}_{1}}\times{{M}_{2}}
, and let
A\subseteq{{D}_{2}}
. We denote by
\theta{{|}_{A}}
the set
\{(f,g{{|}_{A}})\,|\,(f,g)\in\theta\}
, where
g{{|}_{A}}
denotes the partial function obtained by restricting every variable of
g
to
A
.

Then for the case of partial functions, Proposition
8.5.3
needs to be modified as follows.

Proposition 9.5.2
.

Let
\theta
and
A
be defined as in Notation
9.5.1
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\theta{{|}_{A}}
is a map.

(ii)

\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta
,
g_{1}{{|}_{A}}=g_{2}{{|}_{A}}
.

(iii)

\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta
,
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
,
g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
or neither
g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
nor
g_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is well-defined.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Definitions
6.1.5
and
8.5.2
are generalized to

Definition 9.5.3
.

Let
\theta
and
A
be defined as in Notation
9.5.1
. Let
f\in\operatorname{Dom}\theta
,
n\in{{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}}
and
(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in A^{n}
. If
g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{2}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
,
\forall(f,g_{1}),(f,g_{2})\in\theta
, then we say that
\theta(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
is
well-defined
and let
\theta(f)(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=g_{1}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})
.
